## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for each "good" item delivered (one color, say BLUE side)
- Score increases by 1 for each "bad" item delivered (other color, say GREEN side)
- We want to: go to the correct source (BLUE side), pick up items, return to nest (WHITE floor), repeat
- Avoid the wrong source (GREEN side)

**Key perceptions:**
- Floor: BLACK = source area, WHITE = nest, GRAY = transit
- Wall colors: BLUE (good source), GREEN (bad source), RED (nest wall)
- Camera colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

**Champion analysis:**
- State 0: GoAwayColor(cyan/6) - moving away from cyan
- State 1: GoToColor(yellow/4) - moving toward yellow
- State 2: Attraction - with white floor trigger back to state 0
- State 3: Attraction - with neighbor count trigger

The champion scores -1.66. Let me think about what would work better.

**Target behavior:** 
- Explore to find source
- Go toward BLUE wall (color 3) to get good items
- Go away from GREEN wall (color 2) to avoid bad items  
- Return to nest via RED wall (color 1) or WHITE floor

**Strategy for machines:**
1. **Elitism**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation**: Small tweaks to champion parameters
3. **Exploration**: New topologies focused on Blue-source foraging

Let me design focused machines:
- State A: Go toward Blue (clr=3) to collect good items
- State B: Go toward Red/nest (clr=1) when on BLACK floor (carrying item)
- State C: Exploration when on GRAY

For the champion, yellow=4 might be the nest LED color robots are using to signal. Let me keep that logic but also try explicit blue/red navigation.

## Reasoning

**Champion Analysis (-4.75):**
- State 0: GoToColor(blue/3) - heading toward blue walls (good source)
- State 1: GoToColor(red/1) - heading toward red wall (nest)
- State 2: Exploration (rwm=15) - random walk

Transitions:
- State 0 → State 1: on BLACK floor (p=0.9) [picked up item, now go to nest]
- State 0 → State 1: on WHITE floor (p=0.5) [already at nest, go to nest? redundant but keeps state 1]
- State 1 → State 0: on WHITE floor (p=0.9) [delivered, now go back to source]
- State 1 → State 1: on GRAY floor (p=0.15) [stay in nest-return mode]
- State 1 → State 0: on BLACK floor (p=0.05) [if at source, return to blue-seeking]
- State 2 → State 0: on BLACK floor (p=0.5) [found source, go blue]
- State 2 → State 1: on GRAY floor (p=0.1) [random transition]

This is elegant: GoToBlue → (on black) → GoToRed(nest) → (on white) → GoToBlue. The exploration state handles when robots get stuck.

**Exploitation strategies:**
1. Keep exact champion
2. Tweak probabilities slightly (p0x0: 0.9→0.95, p1x0: 0.9→0.95)
3. Add more transitions to handle edge cases
4. Try different rwm values for exploration state
5. Try GoAwayColor(green) instead of exploration

**Exploration strategies:**
- 4-state version: add a dedicated "avoid green" state
- Use GoAwayColor(green/2) when perceiving green
- Try different color combinations
- Add neighbor-based transitions
- Try 2-state simplified version

Key insight: the champion already has the right loop (blue→nest→blue). Let me exploit this heavily.

## Reasoning

**Champion Analysis (-12.50):**
- State 0: GoToColor(blue/3) - heading toward blue walls (good source)
- State 1: GoToColor(red/1) - heading toward red wall (nest)

Transitions:
- State 0 → State 0: on BLACK floor (p=0.9) [at source, stay in blue-seeking... wait, destination is state 0 itself? No - with 2 states, leaving state 0, the only other state is state 1, so D=0 means state 1!]

Wait, let me re-read: "With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0."

So:
- State 0 → State 1: on BLACK floor (p=0.9) [at source/black floor → go to nest]
- State 0 → State 1: on WHITE floor (p=0.6) [at nest → go to nest? Hmm, or stay cycling]
- State 1 → State 0: on WHITE floor (p=0.9) [at nest/delivered → go to blue source]
- State 1 → State 0: on BLACK floor (p=0.05) [at source → go to blue source]

This is very clean! State 0 = go to blue source, triggered by black floor → switch to state 1 (go to nest). State 1 = go to red/nest, triggered by white floor → switch to state 0 (go to blue). The score of -12.50 is excellent.

**Exploitation:**
1. Keep exact champion
2. Tweak p0x0 (0.9→0.95), p0x1 (0.6→0.7), p1x0 (0.9→0.95), p1x1 (0.05→0.03)
3. Add a 3rd transition to handle GRAY floor
4. Try different probability combinations

**Key insight:** The 2-state machine is very elegant. Small tweaks to probabilities may improve it further. Also, adding a transition for GRAY floor in state 1 (to keep going to red) might help.

**Exploration:**
- 3-state versions adding exploration or green-avoidance
- Try GoAwayColor(green) as middle state
- Try higher/lower probabilities

- State 1 → State 0: on WHITE floor (p=0.9) [delivered item, go back to blue source]
- State 1 → State 0: on BLACK floor (p=0.05) [at source, go to blue]
- State 1 → State 1: perceives green (p=0.8) [seeing green, stay in nest-return mode? Or this triggers GoAwayColor state?]

Wait - State 1 transition n1x2 has destination 1, condition 7 (perceives color), l1x2=2 (green). But destination index 1 with 3 states leaving state 1... The other states are [0, 2], so D=1 means state 2!

So:
- State 1 → State 2 (GoAwayGreen): when perceives green (p=0.8) [avoid green source]
- State 2 → State 1 (GoToRed): on BLACK floor (p=0.85) [escaped green, go to nest]
- State 2 → State 0 (GoToBlue): on GRAY floor (p=0.2) [on gray, go find blue]

This is clever! The green-avoidance state prevents collecting bad items.

**Strategy:** This machine is excellent. Exploit heavily with small tweaks:
1. Exact champion copy
2. Tweak p values (0.9→0.95 for key transitions)
3. Try p0x0 higher (0.9→0.95) to switch to nest faster
4. Try different rwm for exploration variants
5. Add a 4th state for explicit exploration

**Key parameters to tune:**
- p0x0: 0.9 (black→nest) - try 0.92, 0.95
- p0x1: 0.6 (white→nest) - try 0.65, 0.7
- p1x0: 0.9 (white→blue) - try 0.92, 0.95
- p1x1: 0.05 (black→blue) - try 0.03, 0.07
- p1x2: 0.8 (perceive green→avoid green) - try 0.85, 0.9
- p2x0: 0.85 (black→nest from avoid) - try 0.9
- p2x1: 0.2 (gray→blue) - try 0.15, 0.25

## Reasoning

**Champion Analysis (-19.95):**
- State 0: GoToColor(blue/3), LED magenta - seek blue source
- State 1: GoToColor(red/1), LED yellow - return to nest
- State 2: GoAwayColor(green/2), LED off - avoid green source

Transitions:
- S0 → S1: BLACK floor (p=0.95) [entered source → go to nest]
- S0 → S1: WHITE floor (p=0.65) [at nest → go to nest mode]
- S1 → S0: WHITE floor (p=0.95) [delivered → seek blue]
- S1 → S0: BLACK floor (p=0.04) [at source → seek blue]
- S1 → S2: perceives green (p=0.85) [sees green → avoid it]
- S2 → S1: BLACK floor (p=0.9) [on black after avoiding → go to nest]
- S2 → S0: GRAY floor (p=0.18) [on gray after avoiding → seek blue]

This is a well-tuned machine. The score improved from -18.37 to -19.95. Let me try to push further.

**Key observations:**
- The green-avoidance state is critical (S1→S2 when seeing green)
- High probabilities (0.95) for floor transitions work well
- The champion is near-optimal for this topology

**Exploitation strategies:**
1. Exact champion copy
2. Try p0x0=0.98, p0x1=0.7 (faster switching on black)
3. Try p1x2=0.9 (more aggressive green avoidance)
4. Try p2x0=0.92, p2x1=0.15 (tune avoidance exit)
5. Add perceive-blue trigger from state 0
6. Add perceive-green trigger from state 0 → state 2 directly

**Exploration:**
- Add a 4th state: when on WHITE floor in S0, go to S3 which is GoToColor(red) more aggressively
- Try GoAwayColor(green) as state 0 (start by avoiding green, then seek blue)
- Try S2 with additional transition: perceive green → stay in S2

**Important note:** In S1 transitions, D=1 maps to state 2 (the other states from state 1 are [0,2], so D=0→state0, D=1→state2). Let me verify my understanding is correct for the champion.

With 3 states, from state 1, other states are [0, 2]:
- n1x0: D=0 → state 0 ✓
- n1x1: D=0 → state 0 ✓  
- n1x2: D=1 → state 2 ✓

From state 2, other states are [0, 1]:
- n2x0: D=1 → state 1 ✓
- n2x1: D=0 → state 0 ✓

Good, my understanding is correct.

## Reasoning

**Situation:** The champion has been stuck at -20.05 for two rounds. The 3-state topology (GoToBlue → GoToRed → GoAwayGreen) is well-tuned. We need to either find better parameters or try genuinely different structures.

**What's working:**
- State 0: GoToColor(blue/3) → switch on BLACK(0.97) or WHITE(0.68)
- State 1: GoToColor(red/1) → switch on WHITE(0.97), BLACK(0.03), or perceive-green(0.88)
- State 2: GoAwayColor(green/2) → switch on BLACK(0.92) or GRAY(0.16)

**Why might we be stuck?**
- The green-avoidance state (S2) exits on BLACK floor (p=0.92) going to nest. But if a robot is in S2 and lands on black (source area), it might be picking up green items before escaping.
- The WHITE floor trigger from S0→S1 (p=0.68) might be causing unnecessary state changes when at nest.
- Maybe S2 should exit on WHITE (delivered already) rather than BLACK.

**New ideas to try:**
1. Try S2 exit on WHITE floor (go to blue) instead of BLACK
2. Try S2 exit on GRAY floor (go to blue) at higher probability
3. Add perceive-blue trigger from S2 → go to S0 (GoToBlue)
4. Try making S0 also detect green and go to S2
5. Try different LED colors to test if signaling matters
6. Try 4-state with explicit "carry-and-return" state
7. Try having S1 also react to WHITE floor → stay in S1 (currently only transitions away)

**Parameter exploration around champion:**
- Try p2x1 (gray→blue) higher: 0.2, 0.25, 0.3
- Try p1x2 (perceive green→avoid) higher: 0.92, 0.95
- Try p0x1 (white→nest) lower: 0.5, 0.4 (less eager to go to nest when already there)

**Key structural variant:** What if S2 (GoAwayGreen) exits to GoToBlue on perceiving BLUE (meaning it's escaped and sees the good source)?

## Reasoning

**Situation:** Champion stuck at -20.05 for three rounds. The 3-state topology is clearly strong but we've exhausted fine-tuning around it. We need more aggressive exploration while maintaining the champion.

**Deep analysis of the champion:**
- S0 (GoToBlue): exits on BLACK(0.97) or WHITE(0.68) → both go to S1
- S1 (GoToRed): exits on WHITE(0.97)→S0, BLACK(0.03)→S0, perceive-green(0.88)→S2
- S2 (GoAwayGreen): exits on BLACK(0.92)→S1, GRAY(0.16)→S0

**Problem identification:** When S2 exits on BLACK floor (p=0.92) going to S1 (GoToRed/nest), the robot might have just been pushed away from green and is now on black floor near green source — going to nest is correct. But p2x1 (gray→blue, 0.16) is low — robots may spend too long in S2 on gray floor.

**What hasn't been tried:**
1. S2 exiting on WHITE floor → go to S0 (if robot reaches nest while avoiding green, restart)
2. S1 also perceiving BLUE → go to S0 (when seeing blue source from nest area)
3. Completely different 3-state topology: GoAwayGreen → GoToBlue → GoToRed
4. Using GoToColor(cyan/6) for nest (cyan LEDs = other robots near nest?)
5. The champion uses LED magenta(5) on S0 and yellow(4) on S1 — robots may be using these to signal location. Try matching/mismatching.
6. Try p2x1 much higher (0.4, 0.5) so robots don't linger in avoid-green state
7. Try S1 perceiving blue → switch to S0 (when near blue, go get it)

**New structural ideas:**
- 4-state: S0=explore, S1=GoToBlue, S2=GoToRed, S3=GoAwayGreen
- Alternative 3-state: start in GoAwayGreen, transition based on floor
- Try making S2 exit on PERCEIVE-BLUE → go to S0 (more directed recovery)

**Key insight I missed:** Maybe the issue is that when robots are in S1 (GoToRed/nest) and they perceive green (p=0.88), they switch to S2 (GoAwayGreen). But what if they're already heading to the nest with a blue item? They abandon their path. Perhaps the green-avoidance should only trigger when on BLACK or GRAY floor, not when heading to nest already.

## Reasoning

**Final round - last chance to beat -20.05.**

The champion has been locked at -20.05 for 4 rounds. The 3-state topology (GoToBlue → GoToRed → GoAwayGreen) is clearly near-optimal for this structure. I need to try both:
1. Very precise fine-tuning of the champion
2. Genuinely different approaches that might unlock better performance

**What I haven't tried systematically:**
- The champion's S2 exits on BLACK(0.92)→S1 and GRAY(0.16)→S0. What if S2 exits on GRAY→S1 (go to nest) instead of GRAY→S0? If the robot avoided green and is on gray, it might have a blue item from before entering green — going to nest makes sense.
- What if S0 (GoToBlue) doesn't exit on WHITE floor at all? The WHITE exit (0.68) might be counterproductive — if robots are at the nest, they should stay in GoToRed mode anyway.
- The S1 perceive-green trigger (0.88) might fire too often even when heading home — try lower probability.
- Try GoToColor(cyan/6) for nest instead of red — maybe cyan marks the nest wall better in some maps?
- Try a version where S2 exits ONLY on perceive-blue (not floor-based): when GoAwayGreen robot sees blue, switch to GoToBlue.
- Try completely different 2-state variant using the champion's insight: GoToBlue + GoToRed with green-perception trigger.

**Parameter grid around champion:**
- p0x0: 0.97 (fixed, very high - keep)
- p0x1: 0.68 → try 0.0 (remove white trigger), 0.3, 0.5, 0.8
- p1x2: 0.88 → try 0.6, 0.7 (less green-avoidance)
- p2x0: 0.92 → try 0.97, 0.98
- p2x1: 0.16 → try 0.5, 0.6 (faster escape to blue)

**Most promising new idea:** Remove the WHITE floor trigger from S0 (set p0x1 very low or change condition). When robots are at the nest (white floor), they should be in S1 anyway. The 0.68 trigger from S0 on white might cause premature switching before item pickup.

Actually wait - on WHITE floor in S0 means the robot went to GoToBlue but ended up at nest (wrong direction). Switching to GoToRed at that point makes sense to reset. Keep it but try lower values.

**Another key idea:** Try S2 with perceive-blue exit → go to S0 (when avoiding green, if you see blue, go get it).

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
